There i s considerable similarity i n the movement i n r e t a i l prices in four countries between these significant periods; with the exception that he last period shown - 1945-48 - the upward movement i n retail prices has very much lower i n New Zealand and Australia than in Canada or the United Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 29 States. The increases i n r e t a i l prices between 19 38 and 1948 (covering the war and the post-war years to date) i n each country are:- New Zeal and,38 per cent.; Australia,44 per cent.; Canada,52 per cent.; and United States,70per cent. The graphical presentation of the movement i n r e t a i l prices over the period 1907 to 1949 i s given on the next two pages.The f i r s t diagram has been plottedonan arithmetic scale which accords equal value to like movements over the whole scale. The second diagram which has been plotted on a ratio (or logarithmic) scale accords to equal proportionate movements, equal value over the whol e seal e. By way of example, movements from 400 to 500, and from 800 to 900 are accorded equal value on an arithmetic scale (100 points in each case) . The relative increases are 25 per cent i n the f i r s t case (100 points on 400) and 12& per cent in the second case (100 points on 800) and on a ratio scale the l a t t e r movement would be given h a l f the value of the former. Equal movement on a ratio scale would be accorded to increases from say 400 to 500 and 800 to 1000 (25 per cent i n each case) . Each of the diagrams i s of value in interpreting the course of p r i c e movements over the period quoted. [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 31 32 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 APPENDIX 11 ( T E C H N I C A L ) INTRODUCTORY As indicated i n the f i r s t part of this Report the method of compilat-ion of the index follows the recommendations of the Index Committee given in Parliamentary JPaper H.48, 1948, to which reference shouldbemade for a statement of the principles followed. However, since the final determination of detail could be made only when the p r i c e collection for the f i r s t quarter of 1949 was complete, i t was thought advisable to place on record details of the method of compilation of the index in such a form as to be comprehensible without reference to the Report of the Index Committee. Again, in the course of the very exten-sive research necessary i n order to i n i t i a t e the index, several interesting technical points came to l i g h t which are worth recording. The f i r s t portion of the Report is^however, self contained, as i t i s realised that the technical details of compilation w i l l be of less interest to the general reader than to students of index numbers and to index number technicians. \"BASE\" OF THE INDEX This expression is often loosely used to cover several distinct ideas, so that a definition of the term i s necessary before such a statement as that the baseof an index i s \"First quarter 1949 (= 1000)\" can be rightly interpreted. Originally the base of a time series of index numbers was the date to which every other date, at which the index was calcul ated, was d i r e c t l y related. In the case of price index numbers prices may be determined at a date, but the quantities used for weights must be averages over a period, and so often are base prices. W e may speak therefore of a base period rather than a base date. Current prices too are usually taken as representing average prices over aperiod. In the case of Paasche's formula, for instance* 1' , a l l the periods (1, 2, 3, n, ) are directly related to the period 0 (not to each other), a new \"base aggregate\" having to be calculated for every determination of prices and quantities (the weights being, of course, current quantities) . With L asp eyres' formula, however, which employs fixed weights* 2 ) the connection o f any period with the base period i s scarcely closer, mathematically, than with any other period. Nevertheless the idea of a base period i s s t i l l of great practical importance, because the weights used belong to that period and s t r i c t l y to no other, as i s indicated by the subscript 0 to the weighting symbol q^ i n the formula. Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 33 It is legitimate then to distinguish two main senses i n which the term \"base\" i s used. (l) The weighting base. 0 2 ) The arithmetical base. In the case of the Consumers' P r i c e Index the weighting base i s the year 1946. It was for this period that the exp enditure p attern was determined. I f theprices i n i t i a l l y applied to this base had also related to the year 1946, the weighting base would also have been the arithmetical base, that is the period i n which a l l the group index numbers and the all-groups index number are equal to 1000. But this was not so; conversely the expenditure pattern for the f i r s t quarter of 1949 has not been determined, but has only been estimated on the assumption that consumption habits have remained unchanged since 1946. The determined pattern for 1946 really constitutes the (weighting) base of the index, but the arithmetical base i s the first\"quarter of 1949* 1*. Series of index numbers are very frequently converted to arithmetical bases other than those on which they were o r i g i n a l l y calculated. For example a series of index numbers from 1907 to 1949 i s included i n the table on page 25 of this Report, a l l related to the arithmetical base : f i r s t quarter, 1949 (= 1000) . As indicated on p age 26,the index number series on which this con-tinuous series i s based have, in themselves, different weighting bases, namely 1909-13, 1926- 30, and the year 1946. The original arithmetical bases were 1909-13, 1926-30, and the f i r s t quarter, 1949 respectively. Had constant weights been used throughout, the results would not have been i d e n t i c a l ; although, i n fact, the l i n k i n g of series with different weighting patterns, as has been done, w i l l give a more r e a l i s t i c picture of price movements over a long period than wouldbethe case i f a single weighting pattern were used, involving as i t would the assumption that l i v i n g p at terns had remained constant over the l a s t forty years. The conversion of index numbers to new \"bases\" i n this way i s v a l i d ( 2 ) , subject to the qualification given earlier that, as the current period becomes remote from that i n which the weighting pattern has been determined, the index becomes a less accurate measure of movements i n r e t a i l prices. A p r a c t i c a l point involved i n the foregoing discussion i s that while current r e t a i l prices are easily collected, quantities become available only-after a lapse of t i m e < s > . The index s t a t i s t i c i a n has therefore to .choose between (1) commencing his collection of prices in advance of the determination of weights, i n which case no index numbers w i l l be capable of calculation for some considerable time, i . e . until the weights are obtained, or (2) back-dating the commencement of the index, i n which case the h i s t o r i c a l portion of i t w i l l be based on research into past prices (and these can never be ascertained with the same accuracy as present prices), or (3) separating the arithmetical base from the weighting base, which i s what has been done i n the present instance. Method (2) would, however, have been feasible had not so many new items been introduced into the regimen. (1) So that the formula becomes:-. . ZPn q 0 \" £ P x % (2) Note however that i f in converting the base each'group index is equated to 1000 the (arithmetical) group-weighting pattern of the index is thereby changed. (3) This fact alone precludes the use of Paasche's formula (unmodified) for current r e t a i l price indices, which, to serve their purpose, have to be released without delay. 34 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 LIMITS OF ERROR Stress has just been l a i d on the importance of accuracy in the prices entering into p r i c e index numbers, and i t i s generally accepted that a higher standard of accuracy i s necessary as .regards prices than as regards weights. An examination into the effect on the a l l groups index nuniber of a small error i n (l) the p r i c e and (2) the weight of any item may be useful. The same argument applies to groups as to items, i f \"group index number\" is substit-uted for \"price of item\", and \"group weight\" for \"commodity weight\". (a) The effect of an error i n price i s exactly similar to that of a price movement. The ratio of the percentage error in the ( a l l groups) index number to the percentage p r i c e error (small or otherwise) inducing i t , i s the ratio of the current expenditure product for the item incorrectly priced to the current aggregate expenditure, which ratio may be called the fractional current expenditure weight of that i t e m ( 1 ) . If the percentages of both index number and price are calculated on base values, this ratio becomes equal to the fractional base expenditure weight of the i t e m ( 2 ) . Thus as the percentage base expenditure weight of the seasonal group i s 4.9, an error of 2h per cent, i n the group index number w i l l produce an error of approximately 14 points in 1000 in the a l l groups index number. (b) The ratio of the percentage error i n the ( a l l groups) index number to the percentage weight error (supposed small) inducing i t , i s equal to the excess of the fractional current expenditure weight over the fractional base expenditure weight of the item incorrectly weighted; which may be expressed as the fractional base expenditure weight of this item multiplied by the excess of the p r i c e relative for the item over the (all groups) index number expressed as a fraction of the (all groups) index number < 3 ). The ratio of the error in the ( a l l groups) index number expressed as a percentage of i t s base value to the percentage weight error (supposed, small) inducing i t , i s simply the fractional base expenditure weight of the item incorrectly weighted, multiplied by the excess of the price relative for the item over the ( a l l groups) index number ( 4 ). Thus an error in the seasonal group expenditure weight would have no effect on the a l l > groups index number, u n t i l the group index number diverged from the a l l groups index number, the effect being then (i) di and di _£n dP„ X 1 ZPn % ZPo % HPn % (2) di , VQ d P n * - r ZPo % (3) (4) di _ di &% % Pr£Poqo - v£.Vn% £ p 0 q 0 > a Pn«* n^o EP n*o EPo % Polo EPo^o P, - i Pq % o % z.p Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 35 for small errors proportionate to the percentage divergence. I f the seasonal group index number werenot more than 20per cent, above or below the a l l groups index number, the effect of a2i per cent, error in the group weight would be of the order of not more than 4 point i n 1000. METHOD OF CALCULATION Retail price indices in nearly every country today use Laspeyres' formula and there seemed no sufficient reason why New Zeal and practice should hold to geometric means as employed in the base 1926-30 series. Any suggestion that the use of unweighted geometric means dispenses with the necessity for weights i s entirely i l l u s o r y . In a comparison of the two methods i t may be observed:-(a) Laspeyres' formula has no \"bias\"; but the method of weighted geometric means (using base expenditure weights) has, assuming theprice relatives show dispersion, a downward bias, whether prices are r i s i n g or f a l l i n g . (b) The aggregative method greatly f a c i l i t a t e s the tracing to i t s source or sources of any movement in an index number. I t i s a simple matter to construct a \"ready reckoner\" by which the effect on (e.g.) the a l l groups index number of a known or postul ated movement in t h e p r i c e of any single item in the index can readily be ascertained. (c) By the use of modern calculating machines which rapidly multiply and accumulate products, great economy in time can be effected in working out aggregates, as against the tedious consultation of logarithmic tables. As w i l l be seen later, the aggregate expenditure method is used for every group index i n every town and for combining a l l groups. In fact each group and all-groups index for each town i s directly related to the respective group or all-groups base aggregate expenditure shown i n Table 12. Thus a l l regional all-groups index numbers are directly comparable one with another and w i l l give an indication of relative price levels i n the various towns as well as relative movements i n prices. (The same i s true of regional group index numbers). However, although the major seasonal influences are smoothed i n the compilation of the indices some seasonal factors of importance remain (see the section on seasonal items given l a t e r ) , and these may vary as between different l o c a l i t i e s . Consequently inter-urban comparisons of l i v i n g costs should be based on the index numbers over a considerable period. Even then careful interpretation of the figures i s necessary. WEIGHTING The weighting pattern of the Consumers' P r i c e Index i s set out in detail in Table 1 2 . Since the weights for items which are represented but not priced are spread over the items priced the method produces a properly weighted series for each group and a l l groups combined without the separate use of a system of group weights. (The actual base group exp enditure p er cent ages have already been shown on page 8 of this Report). The constant changing 36 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 of spending patterns i s the most cogent reason why price index series require frequent revision. Not only has the schedule of commodities and services i t s e l f to he reviewed, but the relative expenditures on a l l items included -new andold - must, on account of these changes i n consumption habits, be worked out afresh. With a general advance i n the standard of l i v i n g greater quant-i t i e s of semi-luxuries in proportion to essentials are consumed. In the case of alternative goods or u t i l i t i e s (e.g. gas and electricity) a competitive price for one may oust the other from the favour of the purchasing public. New discoveries or inventions (e.g. radio, nylon, labour-saving machines, air travel, television) may rapidly supplant older habits or arouse a demand hith-erto latent. Old sources of supply becoming depl eted or closed and new sources being opened may compel changed spending habits. Fashions too are f i c k l e , but command an extensive following. By 1948 then, neither the 1926-30 pattern nor that of 1942 could be considered applicable any longer. Theoretically a revision should be under-taken at a time of stable economic conditions, but in practice the attainment of some measure of relative s t a b i l i t y must suffice. The new index is based on average New Zealand consumption habits during the year 1946 with particular reference to urban dwellers. (At the time this research was undertaken 1947 figures were not complete). National consumption was taken as: national production, plus imports, less exports, less quantities consumed or further processed i n manufacturing operations. Thus for coal the residual quantity, after allowing for commercial consumption in factories, by shipping, and on railways, was taken as domestic consumption. The quantity of sugar was re-duced by the amount used in Jams, confectionery, cakes, etc., these \"processed\" commodities coming into the index in their own places. Similarly the weight of flour was merely that purchased as such at r e t a i l , and excluded flour used i n bread and other bakery products as well as i n numerous other ways. The general method was adapted as required to suit special cases. This was p a r t i c u l a r l y necessary as regards clothing and footwear, the total cost of which on the r e t a i l market could be ascertained with reasonable accuracy, but not the dissection into the items selected for inclusion i n the^index. A sample inquiry was made among a number of selected households; by this means relative expenditures on- the index items were arrived at and the ascertained total expenditure broken up accordingly. From these expenditure weights, quantity weights were later calculated as described below. In many cases (not only as regards apparel but generally) values of factory output or imports were available, but not quantities. It was mainly in the clothing and foot-wear group, however, that the 1 ack of information as to quantities was a source of d i f f i c u l t y . Generally quantities and prices were known for index items, and for others values were sufficient. These values of extra-index items had to be spread over the values of the index items, either by allocation or by apportionment. Thus the expenditure on lamb was allocated to mutton, but that on poultry apportioned over the whole meat group. Actually these adjustments were made at a r e t a i l price level., quantities and known r e t a i l prices readily producing r e t a i l values. In cases where quantities were not available values were marked up from factory door or import value to r e t a i l by a percentage calculated for a l l i e d items which showed both quantity and (factory door or import) value. The adjusted values of index items, after making allowance for items not priced, were eventually reconverted to quantities by dividing Abstract of Statistics, 30t/i November, 1949] 37 by 1946 r e t a i l prices. This was not exactly however the original intention which was rather to bring the expenditures to a f i r s t quarter, 1949 level and then divide by f i r s t quarter, 1949 prices. The general method of calculating the index weights was therefore: -(a) Ascertainment of physical national consumptions during 1946. (b) Conversion of these quantities to expenditures, using average 1946 r e t a i l prices. (c) Adjustment of expenditures so obtained by bringing in expenditures on items which i t was desired to represent in the index, but which were not suitable for pricing- Mostly these represented groups of items of which none was of special importance (e.g. groceries such as J e l l y crystals, canned andpacket soups, flavouring essences, spices, macaroni, spaghetti, yeast, fruit extract, cordials, candied peel, sandwich spreads, etc.). As noted above some classes of expenditure are not represented i n the index, e.g. the weight for aerated waters does not include any adjustment on account of alcoholic drinks. (d) As indicated earlier, a complete weighting pattern was compiled and included i n the Report of the Index Committee based on the latest inform-ation as to prices available at the time the Report was prepared (June 1948). Normally a weighting pattern based on this period would have been sufficiently accurate for a l l practical purposes. There were however, in fact, quite anumber of p r i c e changes between June, 1948 and the f i r s t quarter of 1949; and, accordingly, i t was deemed advisable to abandon the predetermined expenditure weights, and to work out the actual base aggre-gates at prices ruling i n the f i r s t quarter of 1949. (The Jresults c»f this calculation are shown in detail i n Table 12). The quantity basis of the weighting i s , as stated above, physical national consumption i n 1946, with the adjustment indicated i n (c) above; that i s to say, the original commodity weights could not be used because of the necessity for bringing i n items to be represented in the index but not priced, and also i n the case of foods, of making deductions for meals taken i n restaur-ants, etc. These could be grafted on to (or excised from) priced items on a value or expenditure basis only. Hence the necessity for converting quantities into values, adjusting these values, and then converting back to the quantities to be used as fixed weights. It w i l l be apparent that the resultant quantity weights cannot be l i t e r a l l y interpreted as actual consumptions of the commodities against which they are shown. For instance the regimen apparently indicates that 14ilbs. of madeira cake i s consumed per head of population per annum (See Table 12) . Actually nothing l i k e this quantity of madeira cake i s baked; what i s really implied i s that the annual expenditure of 271.46 pence per head (14.25 lbs. at 19.05 pence per lb.) « n t e r e d o n t h e same l i n e i s approximately correct (at f i r s t quarter, 1949 prices), not for expenditure on madeira cake alone, but for total expenditure on a l l kinds of cake, pastry, buns, and scones. Owing to the d i f f i c u l t y of setting up standards for bakery and home cookery products - a 38 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 necessary preliminary to p r i c i n g them - i t has to he assumed that the prices of a l l such goods move i n agreement with t h e p r i c e of a typical l i n e which i s selected as suitable for pricing, i n this case madeira cake. I t i s not even legitimate to assume that 14.125 lbs. of a l l cake etc. i s consumed annually per person. The price per l b . of some small goods may be much higher than that of madeira cake, and consequently, al though the total exp enditure i s correct, the hypothetical quantity is greater than the actual. This treatment i s not only v a l i d but necessary i f a true aggregate or a true index number i s to be obtained. Even i n an apparently straightforward case such as bread the consump-tion weight cannot be l i t e r a l l y interpreted. The index prices the cheapest form of bread, v i z . the 2-lb. l o a f s e l l i n g at 5&d. i n the larger towns. :For purchases in the form of 1-lb.loaves and \"fancy\" loaves, theprice per l b . ranges from 3d. to over 4d. per l b . , instead of 2fd. Here again, the assumption that a l l expenditure on bread i s at the index price involves an Inflated quantity figure, v i z . the quantity of bread which would be consumed (or at least purchased) i f a l l the money spent on bread were used in buying 2-lb. loaves. In taking the product of the current p r i c e of a 2-'lb. loaf by this quantity as the current expenditure on bread, a l l that i s assumed i s that the prices of other loaves move in proportion to that of the !2-lb. loaf, except of course the general assumption of fixed weights, which supposes that the per capita quantities of a l l the different kinds of bread remain constant. Where p racticabl e the method described above has been adopted through-out the index in determining the fixed quantity weights for the specific commod-i t i e s priced. In some instances - more particularly i n the miscellaneous group - several departures from this procedure were necessary. For some of the commodities and services concerned 1946 prices were not available. In many-cases, e.g.cosmetics, permanent waves, dry cleaning, only approximate estimates of expenditures could be made. However for some miscellaneous items, e.g. newspapers, fares, tobacco, cigarettes, data were available which allowed of the calculation of quantity weights on the same basis as that used i n the case of food. The housing group does not easily lend i t s e l f to the determination of quantity weights. The unit of consumption i s the occupancy of one house per household, but the house i s rented in some cases and owned in-others. On a national or regional level the costs of both types of houses can be combined to give total housing costs, but the attempt to reduce the components to a consump-tion p er capita basis is apt to be somewhat misleading. Generally i t may be stated that any movement i n the cost of specified goods or services (including housing costs) gives rise to a \"price relative\", which i s the ratio of the new cost to the old. The p r i c e relative is in each case \"weighted\" by the prescribed expenditure on the goods or services in question. The total of the new expenditures divided by the total of the old (i.e. the total of the weights) gives a weighted average price relative or index number. Now the process of weighting aprice relative by the prescribed expenditure involves, f i r s t dividing that expenditure by the old cost, and then multiplying the quotient by the new cost. The quotient before multiplying by the new cost i s the (quantity) weight used i n the worksheets. Normally this quantity weight i s capable of expression in units of consumption per capita; but i t is not necessary Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 39 that i t should be so expressed. In the case of housing costs i t i s better not to attempt any such expression, but the v a l i d i t y of the computations Is i n no way affected. Accordingly i n the Housing section of Table 12 the second and third columns are l e f t blank, while the fourth column i s to be read i n conjunc-tion with these notes. FREQUENCY OF COMPILATION OF INDEX NUMBERS Prices of food and fuel and l i g h t i n g are collected from a l l twenty-one towns each month. Prices of seasonal fruits and vegetables are collected from the ten major towns at weekly intervals. P r i c e s t a t i s t i c s for clothing, footwear, and various miscellaneous items are collected at quarterly intervals from the ten major, towns, and for services from the four chief centres only; while the collection of rent s t a t i s t i c s i s made i n February and August of each year from each of the twenty-one towns. The system of collection Of prices is explained on pages5,6,and 7 of this Report. I t should be noted that a l l price quotations for the same item i n each town are averaged arithmetically, no attempt being made to give greater specific importance to the prices quoted by thelarger stores in the busier parts of a t o m . The majority of the returns received from any town, do, however, in fact relate to the busier stores. The following programme of compilation i s followed:-Group Frequency of Compilation Towns Covered Food . . . . •. . . Fuel and l i g h t i n g Housing Clothing, and footwear Miscellaneous commodities and services . . Monthly n Twice yearly (Feb. & Aug) Quarterly (Feb., May, Aug. and Nov.) Twenty-one towns n n n Ten towns Ten towns, or (in some cases) four chief centres. The frequency of compilation of the Index Number for each Group and the towns covered i n each Group Index are shown above. The all-groups Index numbers are compiled at quarterly intervals i n the following manner : -March June September Quarterly index numbers compiled from average of January, February, and March prices for food and fuel and l i g h t i n g , combined with February index numbers for housing, clothing, footwear and miscel-laneous groups. Quarterly index numbers compiled from average of A p r i l , May, and June prices for food and fuel and l i g h t i n g , combined with May index numbers for clothing, footwear, and miscellaneous groups and February index number for housing. Quarterly index numbers compiled from average of July, August, and September prices for food, fuel and lighting, combined with August index numbers for housing, clothing, footwear, and miscellaneous groups. 40 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 December Quarterly Index numbers compiled from average of October, November, and December prices for food and fuel and l i g h t i n g , combined with November index numbers for clothing, footwear, and miscellaneous groups and August index for housing. COMPILATION OF N E W ZEALAND (TWENTY-ONE TOWNS) WEIGHTED AVERAGE INDEX NUMBERS In compiling New Zealand (average twenty-one towns) index numbers the index numbers (or more correctly the group or all-groups aggregates) for each town are weighted by population weights. However, i n forming these population weights the population of neighbouring boroughs and town d i s t r i c t s i s included in each case, the idea being that the index for a town w i l l be weighted by a population representing not only the town i t s e l f but contiguous areas also. As is indicated i n the Immediately preceding paragraphs, index numbers are available for the twenty-one towns only for the Food, Housing, and Fuel and lighting groups. The prices on which the index i s based are urban r e t a i l prices and urban housing costs; whil e the weighting - though based on New Zealand consumption does not give f u l l representation of such items as produce home grown for own use. The index is then essentially an urban one and the New Zealand average index should be interpreted ,in this sense. However, as mentioned earlier (page 8 ) , the index should represent reasonably well changes i n consumer prices in rural l o c a l i t i e s also:, since, as w i l l be seen below, important rural shopping centres are well represented. In compiling New Zealand average index numbers for these groups the following series of population weights is used:-POPULATION WEIGHTS, SERIES A (Applies to Food (except seasonal) , Housing, and Fuel and l i g h t i n g Groups) Popul ation Weights - i . e . To obtain all-groups index numbers for each t o w n i t i s necessary to specifically impute to the smaller towns tne appropriate index for i t s larger neighbour. In practice i t is the aggregates which are imputed. For example, the all-groups aggregate for Whangarei consists of a combination of the Whangarei aggregates for non-seasonal foods, fuel andlighting, and housing, with the Auckland aggregates for seasonal fruits, vegetables, and eggs, clothing and footwear, hardware, drapery, furniture, cleaning supplies, household appliances, personal and educational requisites etc. and services. SEASONAL ITEMS As indicated elsewhere, the weighting of the index i s based on fixed weights, since i t s purpose i s to measure price fluctuations and, to serve this end, a fixed pattern of weighting i s desirable. Fo.r this reason certain items subject to some seasonal variation in prices and/or supplies are included in the index with fixed weights and without any seasonal adjustment of prices. (But see next paragraph re potatoes). Following are the items coming under this heading:- Milk, meats, potatoes, onions, oranges, bananas. Milk andmeats are included in the appropriate sub-groups (\"Other foods\" and \"Meat and fish\" respectively) weighted i n the same manner as any other commodity (see Table 12). Similarly potatoes, onions, oranges and bananas are included i n the \"Fruits, vegetables and eggs\" sub-group, together with other fixed-weight items, i . e . , processed fruits (sultanas, dates, prunes, dried apricots, canned peaches) and processed vegetables (canned and dried peas) . These items in a l l account for somewhat more than one-third of the total weight given to the Fruits, vegetables and eggs group. In the case of potatoes, although fixed weights are used throughout the year (the assumption being that consumption is reasonably constant each month) a special technique is necessary i n establishing a p r i c e . Main crop potatoes are included in the index for the months of March to October inclusive. In November an average price is calculated for potatoes in North Island towns by averaging the price of new and main crop potatoes i n the ratio of: new, '25 per cent., old, 75 per cent. In the South Island main crop potatoes only are used in the November index. In December new and old potato prices are used, using the following ratios - North Island: new, 75 per cent., old, 25 per cent.; South Island: new, 50 per c a i t . , old, 50 per cent.- In January and February prices of new potatoes only are used for both Islands. This rather elaborate technique was recommended by the Index Committee as a practical method of avoiding the d i f f i c u l t y created i n the transitional months for this item, when new and old potatoes are on the market in varying proportions in different parts of the country. (The Committee also recommended that the Government S t a t i s t i c i a n should vary tnis formula when circumstances rendered any variation desirable, e.g. a very l a t e season or a poor main crop: but that a statement should be published on such occasions defining the variations with reasons. (See H.48, 1948, pages 17 and 18). Items of fruits and vegetables other than those mentioned above are considered together as composing a seasonal \"basket\". This basket contains 44 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 fruits and vegetables (e.g. raspberries, french beans) which are subject to wide variations in both supply and price (indeed some items are not available at a l l for several months of the year) and eggs, which also vary considerably in both price and supply at different seasons of the year. Normally such items are not included in consumers* p r i c e index numbers: but in view of their importance i n the d i e t ( 1 ) their inclusion i n the index adds very considerably to i t s value as representing actual household expenditures on food. Some d i f f i c u l t technical problems had to be surmounted in including these items i n the index for the following reasons:-(a) Some fruits and vegetables are not i n continuous supply throughout the year, e.g. small fruits, such as raspberries, strawberries, gooseberries. (b) Even i n cases where seasonal fruits and vegetables are in more or less continuous supply there aremarked seasonal changes in the quantities coming on the market. (c) Considerable purely seasonal changes i n p r i c e s occur; thesemay bep erfectly normal, but i t would obviously be undesirabl e to incorporate sudden changes in the all-groups index duepurely to these seasonal increases and decreases i n prices. Much less should the index take into account the abnormal or out-of-season prices quoted at times wnen very small supplies at scarcity values appear i n the markets (e.g., small quantities of new peas in July-August) . Thepeculiaritiesof these commodities present formidable problems for the index number technician. The methods adopted are outlined in the following paragraphs. In order tomeet the f i r s t two problems outlined in (a) and (b) above, recourse was had to a changing basket of fruits and vegetabl es, the content of the basket being changed from month to month as some fruits and vegetables come into season and others go out of season. Obviously i t would be undesirable to include, i n any month's basket, commodities just coming on to the market i n very small quantities at scarcity prices, which in fact the average housewife woul d no t purch ase until a following month when fuller supplies were available. Consequently in drawing up the contents of each month's basket,.only those com-modities which are i n supply in reasonably adequate quantities i n that month were included, and, further, the varying quantities of each commodity coming on the market from month to month while i t i s i n season had to be considered. Following i s the l i s t of the commodities included i n theseasonalbasket for each month:-Commodity Months Included i n Index Peas Beans -Fr ench Runner Lettuce Cabbage Cauliflower C arrots November-April. E ec emb er- Ap r i l . December- Apr 11. November-May. Whole year, except October. .Whole year. Whole year. 1 ) T h i s seasonal sub-group accounted for Just over 14 per cent, of total expenditure on food in the base period January - March 1949, Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 45 Commodity Parsnips Swedes Beetroot KumaraS Brussels sprouts Pump kin Tomatoes Silver beet Apples Pears Peaches Plums Apricots Lemons, New Grap ef ruit, Raspberries Strawberries Gooseberries Eggs Zeal and New Zealand Months Included i n Index April-October. A p r i l - Sep ternber. Whole year. March-June. May-'Sep tember. Whole year, except Novanber. Dec ember- Ap r i l . Whole year, except October. Whole year. Whole year. December- ftp r i l . Dec ember-Ap r i l . January-February. Whol e y ear. June-October. December-February. November-December. November-December. Whole year. 1. Since the composition of this basket changes from month to month a technique had to be devised which would permit of p r i c e changes i n this changing basket being linked to the average price changes in other commodities with fixed weighting. The method by which this was done was to adjust the quantities in each monthly basket i n such a manner as to obtain a total expenditure on fruits and vegetables i n each month of 1946 (the weighting base of the Consumers' Price Index) which would have been constant throughout the twelve months had therebeenno \"long-term\" movement i n the prices of the seasonal items included. In making this adjustment regard was had, also, to the necessity of maintaining in each month a correct relationship between the quantities of the various commodities which are marketed i n that month. The steps by which these conditions were met follow:-Statistics were obtained showing the actual quantities of a large number of seasonal fruits and vegetables marketed in each month of 1946, together with average r e t a i l market prices i n each month of 1946 and the f i r s t three months of 1947 for the more important items. It was assumed that the average change in the retail price of each item between the f i r s t quarter of 1946 and the f i r s t quarter of 1947 represented the secular or long-term trend i n the p r i c e of that item i n 1946 ( i . e . the change in prioe not attributable to purely seasonal influences) . The actual prices charged in each month were then \"corrected\" for this trend: that i s , thepart of the recorded change i n price between January, 1946 and each successive month due to this long-term trend was eliminated by applying a \"trend factor\", which represented the amount to date of the annual change spread evenly at simple interest over the twelve months. This adjustment produced a series of prices for each month from which long-term trend had been eliminated: but in which purely seasonal changes i n prices were s t i l l included. 3. The quantities marketed each month were multiplied by the monthly average !2. 46 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 prices adjusted as indicated i n (2) above, which produced a series of agg-regate expenditures each month containing two variants, (a) seasonal changes in prices, (b) seasonal changes i n quantities marketed. 4. The twelve monthly totals as obtained i n (3) were added, and the total div-ided by 12. 5. The quantity of each commodity marketed each month was multiplied by the monthly average aggregate as obtained i n (4) , and divided by the actual aggregate for that month as obtained in <3).. This produced a set of quantity weights for each month of 1946 which, when costed at the respective market prices for each month, corrected for. trend in every case, would result i n identical aggregate expenditures for each month of 1946. By this adjustment of the weights the effect of seasonal changes i n prices and quantities marketed i n 1946 had been eliminated, as had the long-term or non-seasonal trend which occurred i n that year ( i . e . , the prices used are a l l at a January, 1946 price level) . 6. The weights as obtained i n (5) were adjusted to a per capita basis; increased slightly to take account of commodities for which no price quotations could be obtained: and also (as the figures represented monthly consumption) multiplied by 12 to put them on a basis comparable with the weights for other groups. This series of quantity weights i s shown i n Table 13. 7. The quantity weights shown in Table 13 ? when multiplied by the actual prices recorded for the fruits, vegetables and eggs priced i n any month and totalled, w i l l produce an \"aggregate expenditure\" for that month which reflects the long-term trend but does not reflect purely seasonal changes in prices i n so far as these changes conform to the 1946 seasonal pattern. 8. Since the \"arithmetical base\" of the Consumers' P r i c e Index i s the f i r s t quarter of 1949, the \"aggregate expenditure\" as obtained in (5) <*>:, was not a suitabl e base-aggregate expenditure for the index. It had to be adjusted for price changes between January 1946 and the f i r s t quarter of 1949. This was done bj, compiling an index for each of the f i r s t three months of 1949 on base: January 1946 ( = 1000); and multiplying the aggregate expenditure referred to above by the simple average of these three index numbers (which was 1467.940) and dividing by 1000. 9. The results of the calculations for each month are shown in detail i n Table 13, which gives, as indicated above, the finally adjusted quantity weight for each item for each month, and the price of each item in each month adjusted as explained i n (8), namely the 1946 price f i r s t reduced to a January, 1946 level and then multiplied by the average index for the f i r s t three months of 1949 on base:January, 1946 (= 1000) and divided by 1000. This produces a series of prices for each month which when multiplied by the quantities shown result i n an i d e n t i c a l aggregate expenditure at f i r s t quarter, 1949 price level for each month (viz., 1568• 67 pence). As indicated earlier this technique does not completely smooth the seasonal items index, since i t eliminates seasonal variations i n price only to the extent that they conform to the 1946 seasonal pattern of price variations. It does, however, provide a method by which these commodities can be included ( 1 5 Which was, as explained, identical for each month of 1946. Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 47 in the index without introducing too large an element of i n s t a b i l i ty, particularly as regards the quarterly all-groups index. The seasonal pattern is not regarded as fixed for any long period of time; and when any marked variations of apparent permanency occur i t w i l l be necessary to recalculate the seasonal adjustment factors. HOUSING As indicated in the Report of the Index Committee (pages 18, 35-36) the Consumers' Price Index takes into account changes i n costs of owner-occupied houses i n addition to rents of unfurnished houses and unfurnished f l a t s . The majority of private houses i n New Zealand are owner-occupied, the proportions at the 1945 Census in the urban areas covered by the Consumers' Price Index being:- Rented, 43 per cent.; o wner-occupi ed, 57 per cent. Since the inclusion of costs of owner-occupied houses i s a novel feature of the Consumers' Price Index, the following notes on the technique adopted are given. * RENTS - Statistics of rents of houses i n twenty-five towns have been collected from house agents (acting as rent collectors) and others by the Census and Statistics Department for a long period of years at six monthly intervals. This collection, continued in the twenty-one towns covered by the Consumers' Price Index, forms the pricing basis for the inclusion of rents of unfurnished houses i n the Consumers' Price Index. The collection of rent s t a t i s t i c s , though extensive, represents a large sample of private rented houses: but returns are available covering a l l State rented houses. A simple average of these figures for each town would result i n considerable overweighting of State houses. To obviate this d i f f i c u l t y estimates were made - based mainly on the 1945 Census of dwellings - of the proportion of private rented to State rented houses in each of the towns covered, the average rents for private rented houses and for State rented houses being weighted by these factors to obtain a weighted average rent for a l l rented houses i n each town. Only houses of four, five, and six rooms are included in the index. Rents of unfurnished flats are obtained from the same sources as rents of unfurnished houses, though, in this case, only two, three, and four roomed flats are included. In order to combine the rentals of houses and flats a series of estimates was made for each town indicating the proportion of rented houses to rented flats. (The final figures vary slightly from the estimates given i n H.48, page 35) . These figures are used as weights to obtain weighted average rents of houses and flats combined. HOUSING COSTS OF OWNER-OCCUPIERS - The items which were taken into consideration i n compiling the index for this group are:-Percentage of Total Costs Return on capital . . . . 56 Depreciation 15 Repairs and maintenance 15 Rates Insurance 11 3 100 48 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 The above weights, by which each of the constituent elements in owner occupiers' housing costs is weighted to obtain an index covering this group, were obtained by use of the f a i r rents formula supplemented by other information obtained^ by the Census and Statistics Department. It should be noted that the item Return on capital\" covers interest on the assessed capital value and as such includes imputed interest on the owner's equity as well as actual outgoings i n interest paid on mortgages. The method of \"pricing\" each item shown above follows:-Return on capital and Deo reel ati on - The capital figure decided on as a basis of assessment of these items was a property of unimproved value £250 and improvements £1, 250 (Report of Index Committee, H.48, page 36), as represent-ing an overall figure for a l l houses i n a l l l o c a l i t i e s . In the Report of the Index Committee (page 36) i t is proposed that return on capital and depreciation, which do not represent cash outgoings, should be represented by static amounts included i n current aggregate expenditures. It has been considered desirable to modify this procedure, in such a manner that the index for this item w i l l take into account changes since thebase period i n interest rates and i n capital housing costs. Since i t i s the overall average i n owner occupiers' costs, and not actual costs of aspecific unchanging group of houses, which i s being con-sidered, the appropriate indicator of ch ange i s not the current rate of interest or of building costs, but the moving averages over a f a i r l y long period. A thirteen-year moving average rate of interest on new mortgages weighted by the amounts secured on town and suburban properties i s being compiled. This w i l l be used as the correcting factor for changes i n interest rates; while as regards building costs a twenty-five-year moving average index, weighted by numbers of private building permits with an adjustment i n respect of houses b u i l t for l e t t i n g , i s considered appropriate. Depreciation i s adjusted annually accord-ing to the movement i n building costs as compiled by the formula given above. Movements in the item \"Return on Capital\" are governed by both changes i n average interest rates and changes in average capital value. (Capital value for this purpose includes a fixed unimproved value of £250 and a figure for improvements - originally £1250 - adjusted by movements in average building costs) . Repairs and Maintenance - A weighting p attern for repairs and main-tenance expenses on owner-occupied houses has been drawn up on the basis of information obtained by the Census and Statistics Department. This indicates as folio ws:-DISTRIBUTION OF REPAIRS AND MAINTENANCE COSTS Per cent. Materials -Paint . . .. . . . . . . 23.21 Wall-paper . . . . . . . . 5.02 Other . . .. . . . . .. 2D. 26 Labour . . . . . . . . . . 51.51 100 .00 Prices are collected for paint and wall-paper. The Wholesale Prices Index for building-materials i s taken as indicative of changes i n costs of materials other than paint and wall-paper; while the o f f i c i a l index number of building wages i s taken as indicating the changes in labour costs. The price relatives for each sub-group - paint, wall-paper, other materials, and labour costs - are weighted by the proportions given above to obtain a single index for repairs and maintenance costs. Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949] 49 Rates - Rates, including special and separate rates are ascertained for a typical housing property. While the valuations used in the case of return on capital and depreciation are taken as the basis of assessment of rates, ad-justments to these values are made, i n practice, according to the size of the town and the date of the last revaluation for rating purposes. Rebates on rates, where allowed, are deducted. Insurance - To cover this item, which includes earthquake risk as well as f i r e insurance, the standard rate for a wooden building in t a r i f f d i s t r i c t C (without loading for contiguity) on the annually adjusted value of improvements (as calculated for depreciation purposes) i s used. Index numbers or p r i c e relatives for the various elements in owner-occupier costs are combined using as weights the percentages given above to arrive at one index of owner-occupiers' costs for each town. This index i s then combined with the index for rents, the weights being approximately i n ratio: owner-occupiers' costs 60, rents 40, i n order to arrive at the housing costs index for each town. The New Zealand average index i s compiled by weighting the individual town indices by the weights given i n a previous p aragraph (Pop-ulation Weights, Series A) . BASE AGGREGATE EXPENDITURE: HOUSING - The base aggregate expenditure ^or housing, shown i n Table 12, was founded on an estimate of the total annual costs of housing - rented and owner-occupied - i n 1946, this aggregate being brought up to the base ( f i r s t quarter, 1949) by using the average change i n rents between 1946 and 1949, as indicative of the change i n housing costs generally during that period. Although housing costs are properly expressed only on a per household basis the figures shown in Table 12 have been convertedto a per head basis i n order to secure uniform treatment with other groups, thus enabling the index for any town or grouping of towns to be compiled throughout a l l groups by the aggre-gate expenditure method. REGIMEN OF CONSUMERS' PRICE INDEX Tables 12 and 13 which now follow set out the complete regimen of the index with base prices and expenditures. Table 12 (pages 53 to 6$ covers a l l groups except seasonal fruits, vegetables and eggs, these receiving separate treatment in Table 13 (pages 67 to 72), which shows the changing regimen from month to month. Table 12 i s arranged i n seven columns. (i) The f i r s t column shows the same l i s t of items as i n H.48, 1948, Appendix B, excep t that toffee has been eliminated and i t s weight transferred to chocol ate. (ii) Column v2) shows the unit of quantity applying to the consumptions i n in Columns (3) and (4) and to the price in Column (5). Thus, under \"Flour\" the consumption figure of 1.67 implies a consumption of 1.67 x 25 (or 41§) l b s . per head per annum, the price quotation (53.59) for the f i r s t quarter of 1949 reading as 4s. 5«.59d. per 25 l b . bag. ( i i i ) In the case of foods the consumption rates shown i n Column (3) are per head of total popul ation. In the Fuel and l i g h t i n g and Miscellaneous groups they are on a \"per household\" basis. In the Clothing and footwear group they D 50 [Abstract of Statistics,\"30th November, 1949 are worked out per head of the appropriate population (men's wear per head of adult males, etc.). (iv) In Column (4) the consumption figures i n the preceding column are reduced to a per capita basis, • i f not already so calculated. Regarding the Housing group, the notes on pages 38-39 apply. Particul ar attention is called to the footnote to Table 12 stating that the consumption figures include allowances for items not priced, as explained on pages 36-37. (v) The base prices appearing i n Column (5) were calculated by the method set out on page 39. In the case of items included in monthly collections the three months were averaged arithmetically. For every item a weighted arithmetic average price (by population weights, series A, B,. or C, as the case might be) was then taken out. These prices are a l l in pence. (vi) The base prices shown in Column (5), in conjunction with the fixed weights shown in Column (4), determine the base expenditure pattern of the index which i s given i n Columns (6) and (7). The figures i n Column (6) are simply products of the corresponding prices and weights appearing i n the two previous columns. They are therefore i n pence, the total affording an estimate of per capita expenditure represented i n the index at first-quarter, 1949 prices. As current prices diverge from base prices, this total w i l l diverge from i t s base value of 32038 .05 pence. The all-groups index number simply measures the extent of that divergence, from time to time or from place to place, the current total being divided by the base total and multiplied by 1000. (vii) Column (7) is a revision of the \"parts per m i l l i o n \" expenditures shown in Parliamentary Paper H.48, 1948, Appendix B, according to the data provided i n Column (6) . They are now, however, expressed in percentages with two decimal places, the last two figures being accordingly omitted. The use of Column (7) as a \"ready reckoner\" may be enlarged on a l i t t l e . It follows from page 34 that the ratio of the percentage movement i n the (all-groups) index number to the percentage price movement inducing i t is the fractional expenditure weight of the item showing the price movement. Thus i f anitem with an expenditure weight of x$ rises i n p r i c e by y$ the (all groups) index number w i l l thereby rise by x$ of y$. The percentages may be reckoned either (a) a l l on current values, or (b) a l l on base values; method (b) being usually more convenient. For example, i t i s stated on page 11 that the change from the summer to the winter price of milk was sufficient to raise the all-groups index by 3.4 points (i.e. from 1000 to 1003.4). This was calculated as follows:-Weighted average price of milk, 21 towns, 1st quarter, 1949, (Table 12, column 5) . . . . .. . . 6.25d. per quart. Weighted average price of milk, 21 towns, 2nd quarter, 1949 . . . . .. . . •• 7.00d. per quart. Increase, 2nd quarter,over 1st 0.75d. per quart. 0 .75 in O — i orf Percentage increase .. . . •• •• 6.25 x Percentage expenditure weight of milk (Table 12 column 7) 2.83 Hence percentage movement in index i s 2.83$ of 12$, or 0.34$, which is equivalent to a rise of 3.4 points i n 1000. Abstract of Statistics, 30tb November, 1949] 51 I f i n the t h i r d quarter the price of milk were to advance to 7£d. per quart, this would be reckoned as an 8-per cent, rise on the base price of 6.25d. (not a7-per cent, rise on the second-quarter price of 7d.) , and the resultant index movement wouldbe2.83$ of 8$, or 0.23$, i . e . a further rise of 2. 3 points in 1000. By expressing a l l movements as percentages of base values the necessity is obviated for determining current.percentage expenditure weights. (Milk prices having risen more than other prices, the current percentage expenditure weight of this commodity becomes in the second quarter somewhat greater than. 2.83$). It may be added that a similar method can be used for inter-city com-parisons, the argument applying equally as well i n space as in time. The tracing of the effect s of commodity pricemovements on group index numbers, and of movements i n group index numbers on all-groups index numbers, likewise affords no d i f f i c u l t y . The seasonal food group, having a different regimen for each of the twelve months of the year, was omitted from Table 12 and i s shown separately in Table 13. The general arrangement i s similar to that of Table 1 2 except for the amalgamation of columns (3) and (4), which would have been identical through-out. There are however a few points c a l l i n g for special comment. (i) The consumption figures shown for each month have been adjusted by the appropriate monthly expenditure equalization factor and also by making allowance for items not priced. (ii) It w i l l be noticed that the total of the third column for every month isthesame, v i z . 1568.67 pence. Consequently also the total of the last column for every month i s the same, v i z . 4.90 (per cent, of total per capita expenditure) . Both these totals appear at the appropriate place i n Table 12 (Group 1(b), Seasonal). Table 13 may therefore be regarded as furnishing twelve separate dissections of these totals, one for each of the twelve months. (iii) These expenditures are stated to be \"on an annual basis\", though varying from month to month. A l l that i s meant here i s that the monthly weights, and therefore expenditures, have been mul t i p l i e d by 12 to bring this group into alignment with the rest of the index, where consumptions and expenditures are expressed i n quantities or values per annum. (iv) The fact that the total of the expenditure column is the same for every month affords proof that the monthly expenditure equalization factorsji ave served their purpose. Nevertheless i t should be immediately added that this result has been secured only by keeping the entire schedule of prices for the twelve months i n exact proportion to that of the 1946 prices (January level) used i n working out those equalization factors. (v) In order to put the ^equalized) monthly expenditure totals for the seasonal group on the same basis as the remainder of the index, the price level has been changed from January, 1946 to f i r s t quarter, 1949. In determining the latter level actual prices collected during the f i r s t quarter of 1949 were, of course, used, but the prices shown in Table 13 are not these actual prices; they are 1946 prices adjusted en bloc for trend to a f i r s t quarter, 1949 level. (vi) The method of calcul ating seasonal group index numbers i s , fundamentally, to compare the current month with the corresponding month of the base year (by 52 [Abstract of Statistics, 30th November, 1949 simple proportion of the total expenditures, the monthly regimen being the same in each case); and then to comp are the corresponding month of the base year with a fixed month (involving an estimation of trend). The adjustment of the weights by the monthly expenditure equalization factor enables these two steps to be taken at once. (vii) To recapitulate: Consumptions shown i n Table 13 have been adjusted (a) for items notpriced, and (b) to equalize monthly expenditures, yet preserv-ing acorrect relation between quantities of the various crops i n season i n any month. Prices are a l l at a first-quarter, 1949 level, but based on the seasonal pattern of 1946. By substituting current prices i n any month for those appearing i n the. table, the aggregate expenditure w i l l be changed from 1568.67 to some other amount. A comparison of this new aggregate with 1568.67 gives a measure of the p r i c e movement for the seasonal group from first-quarter 1949 to the current month. ( v i i i ) There are thus twelve \"ready reckoners\", one for each month. Each can be 'legitimately used for comparing (a) the seasonal group index number for any month with that for the corresponding month of another year, or (b) any town (seasonal group) index number for any month with any other town (seasonal group) index number for the same month (or the corresponding month of another year) . The same method cannot, however, be used to calculate month-to-month or quarter-to-quarter movements i n the all-groups index numbers. n![](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/header/refer.svg?v=COf32pJmwQkkNUku)\n![](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/header/resource-affiliate.svg?v=IYHID-KiD787hLOg)\n![](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/header/support-contact.svg?v=l3zbud8U8G8WClxi)\n![](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/header/support-faq.svg?v=3zu7V9EACofR-J36)\n![](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/header/support-devices.svg?v=UR4wiFt6w835ZLAy)\n\n# Cost of Living in New Zealand: What You'll Really Pay\n\nGet a clear view of the cost of living in New Zealand, from rent to groceries, and learn how an eSIM can help you save on roaming while settling in.\n\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w2000/2026/01/dcaef9526366d554373e6acfab01b27d.png)\n\nThe cost of living in New Zealand affects anyone planning a long stay or a permanent move, and understanding it early can make settling in far smoother. Rent, groceries, utilities, and transport vary widely depending on the city and your lifestyle, and knowing realistic ranges helps you set a budget you can actually stick to. Newcomers often rely on tools that simplify daily logistics, and many choose Jetpac to keep data costs predictable while they get settled and move between cities.\n\nWith the right preparation, living in New Zealand becomes easier to manage both financially and practically.\n\nThe cost of living in New Zealand varies widely depending on where you stay, whether you prefer city life or small-town living, and your lifestyle choices. A single professional living in the city center pays very differently from a family of four in a regional town.\n\nMany international movers also use a [Jetpac](https://www.jetpacglobal.com/) [eSIM](https://www.jetpacglobal.com/product-details/new-zealand-esim/) to keep daily travel effortless and fast, especially when switching between cities or handling online logistics in their first few months. With that groundwork set, let’s break down what you can expect once you arrive.\n\n## What Makes New Zealand Expensive and What Doesn’t\n\nNew Zealand is a remote island nation, and that geographic isolation influences the cost of living in New Zealand more than most newcomers expect. Imported goods such as packaged foods, electronics, furniture, and clothing often carry higher prices due to shipping and distribution costs. Salaries sit within a moderate range, but rising demand in major cities has pushed rental and grocery costs upward in recent years.\n\nAt the same time, some categories surprise people with their affordability. Nature is free and abundant. Public parks, beaches, lakes, and scenic reserves cost nothing to enjoy, making outdoor recreation a major lifestyle advantage. Local produce such as vegetables, dairy, and seasonal fruits can be budget-friendly when purchased from weekly markets. The cost of things in New Zealand that relate to outdoor living, such as hiking or beach access, remains extremely low, which helps balance out other expenses. Overall, the New Zealand cost of living is a mix of high essentials and low-cost lifestyle perks.\n\n## Housing: The Single Largest Expense\n\n### Renting in New Zealand: City vs Small Town Reality Check\n\nHousing is typically the biggest part of the cost of living in New Zealand for individuals and families. Rent varies dramatically from city to city. In Auckland, Wellington, and Queenstown, a one-bedroom apartment in the city center can range from $1200 to $1800 per month, while the same setup in Christchurch, Dunedin, or Invercargill may cost between $900 and $1200. Shared flats remain a popular choice for students and newcomers, costing roughly $150 to $250 per week depending on the neighbourhood. Upfront costs also include a bond of up to four weeks’ rent, possible letting fees, and furniture purchases if the unit is unfurnished.\n\n### Buying a Home: What Expats Should Know\n\nBuying property adds a new layer to the New Zealand cost of living. Median home prices in Auckland frequently exceed $700000, while Wellington and Queenstown follow closely behind. Smaller cities offer more attainable prices, often between $400000 and $600000. Additional costs include mortgage interest, home insurance, and annual council rates. Many migrants wait several years before purchasing because it takes time to understand which region suits their budget, work life, and lifestyle.\n\n### Temporary Housing Options\n\nMost newcomers begin their stay in short-term accommodation while searching for long-term housing. Short-stay hotels, serviced apartments, and Airbnb rentals typically range from $70 to $150 per night outside peak season. Hostels cost between $20 and $35 per night and are popular with young travelers or students. This transition period helps people understand the cost of living in Auckland, New Zealand, or any chosen city before committing to a rental contract.\n\n## Food and Grocery Costs: Everyday Spending You Can’t Avoid\n\n### Supermarket Prices and Monthly Grocery Estimates\n\nThe cost of food in New Zealand tends to be higher than in many countries due to import reliance and local production costs. A weekly grocery basket for one person may range from $60 to $90, depending on dietary choices. A family of four often spends between $180 and $250 per week. Basic staples like bread, milk, pasta, and local produce remain reasonably priced, while items such as cheese, meat, and packaged snacks can increase the average cost of living in New Zealand significantly. Shopping at markets, buying seasonal produce, and comparing supermarket loyalty deals can help reduce expenses.\n\n### Dining Out: Cafés, Takeaways, Restaurants\n\nEating out is part of the local culture, but it adds up quickly. A regular café breakfast costs around $12 to $18, takeaway lunches range from $10 to $14, and a standard restaurant meal averages $18 to $25. Dining out frequently raises the cost of things in New Zealand, especially for students and new residents. Many locals balance restaurant outings with home cooking to maintain reasonable monthly budgets.\n\n## Transport Costs: Getting Around\n\n## Public Transport in Major Cities\n\nPublic transport availability influences the cost of living in New Zealand, depending on where you live. Auckland and Wellington offer bus, train, and ferry services, with average fares ranging from $2 to $5 per trip depending on distance. Monthly passes can cost between $120 and $200. Smaller cities rely primarily on buses, and schedules may be limited outside peak hours. For commuters living outside city centers, transport becomes a major budget consideration.\n\n### The True Cost of Car Ownership\n\nFor many residents, owning a car becomes essential, especially in regional areas. Fuel prices average around $7.60 per gallon. Insurance, maintenance, and annual Warrant of Fitness inspections increase the cost of living in New Zealand cost over time. Parking fees in central Auckland or Wellington add additional expenses. Despite these costs, the convenience and flexibility of having a personal vehicle make it a preferred option for many.\n\n### Alternatives: Biking, Rideshare, Car Share Programs\n\nBiking is popular in flat cities like Christchurch, with rentals starting around $20 per day. Rideshare services cost between $10 and $30, depending on distance. Car share programs charge per hour and are useful for people who only need a car occasionally. These alternatives help manage the cost of living in New Zealand for those avoiding car ownership.\n\n## Utilities, Internet, and Mobile Bills\n\n### Power, Heating, and Water\n\nUtility costs vary based on home size and personal usage. A small apartment may incur monthly power bills between $80 and $120, while larger homes range from $150 to $220. Winter heating increases costs significantly, especially in older homes that lack insulation. Water charges are typically included in rent, except in some regions.\n\n### Internet and Mobile Phone Costs\n\nBroadband plans range from $50 to $80 monthly, depending on speed. Mobile phone plans start around $15 for basic data, but heavy users often spend between $25 and $40 each month. New Zealand eSIM options from brands like Jetpac offer the best packs, while long-term migrants choose traditional plans. For travelers, roaming becomes costly, which is why many look for the [best eSIM for New Zealand](https://www.jetpacglobal.com/product-details/new-zealand-esim/) during short visits.\n\nBelow are the other aspects to consider when planning to live in New Zealand:\n\n## Healthcare and Insurance: What You’ll Actually Pay\n\nNew Zealand’s public healthcare system subsidizes many services but does not eliminate costs. Tourists' visits typically range from $25 to $45, while urgent care appointments cost $60 to $120. Prescriptions average around $3 to $25, depending on medication type. Private insurance is recommended for migrants and students who need broader coverage or faster access to specialists. Monthly insurance premiums range from $40 for basic plans to $120 or more for comprehensive coverage.\n\n## Childcare, Schooling, and Education Costs\n\nChildcare is one of the most significant components of the cost of living in New Zealand for families. Full-day daycare fees range from $650 to $900 per month, depending on location. School expenses include uniforms, stationery, and occasional activity fees costing $150 to $350 annually. University fees for international students range from $18000 to $30000 per year, depending on the program. These education-related expenses influence decisions about where to go and how to budget long-term.\n\n## Entertainment and Lifestyle Costs\n\nEntertainment costs vary widely depending on personal choices. A gym membership averages $35 to $55 monthly. Movie tickets cost around $12 to $16. Weekend activities such as kayaking tours or guided walks range from $20 to $70. Fortunately, New Zealand’s outdoor lifestyle helps offset these expenses since the country’s most beautiful experiences, beaches, mountains, and lakes, remain completely free. This helps reduce the perception that living in New Zealand is expensive when lifestyle spending is balanced.\n\n## City Comparison: Most Affordable vs Most Expensive Regions\n\n### Highest Cost Cities\n\nAuckland, Wellington, and Queenstown remain the most expensive regions. High rent, demand for skilled workers, and tourism-driven pricing increase the cost of living in Auckland, New Zealand, and similar cities. Groceries, transport, and entertainment also cost more compared to smaller towns.\n\n### Lower Cost Cities and Regional Areas\n\nDunedin, Invercargill, New Plymouth, and Palmerston North offer more affordable living conditions. Rent is significantly lower, and commuting costs are reduced due to shorter travel distances. These regions often appeal to students, young families, and workers seeking a lower average cost of living in New Zealand without sacrificing lifestyle.\n\n## Monthly Budget Examples: Realistic Scenarios\n\n### Solo Working Professional\n\nExpect monthly expenses between $1800 and $2500. This includes rent, food, transport, utilities, mobile bills, gym memberships, and occasional outings. These averages reflect the true cost of living in New Zealand for single residents in medium-cost cities.\n\n### Student or Backpacker Staying 6-12 Months\n\nShared housing reduces costs significantly. Students typically spend $1200 to $1600 monthly, including groceries, public transport, and weekend travel. These budgets align with the cost of things in New Zealand that matter to young travelers.\n\n### Family of Three or Four\n\nFamilies spend between $3500 and $4800 per month, depending on childcare, schooling, housing size, and transport. Daycare is one of the biggest contributors to the cost of living in New Zealand for families.\n\n## How to Lower Your Cost of Living in New Zealand\n\nYou can reduce your New Zealand cost of living through practical steps such as buying second-hand furniture, choosing suburbs with shorter commutes, and using loyalty cards at supermarkets.\n\nFuel apps help drivers find the lowest prices, and many free Department of Conservation walking tracks offer world-class scenery at no cost. Shoulder-season travel helps reduce expenses for those exploring the country. These approaches help residents balance whether living in New Zealand is expensive relative to their budget.\n\n## Staying Connected Without Overspending\n\nReliable connectivity is essential for work, navigation, or staying in touch with family abroad. Mobile and data costs vary, and many newcomers compare roaming, prepaid SIM cards, and eSIM options to save money. Travelers moving in and out of the country choose [Jetpac](https://www.jetpacglobal.com/) for prepaid data during international trips, which avoids roaming shocks.\n\nIt is better to be prepared before leaving for New Zealand, so to check whether your phone is compatible or not, you can read the full blog here:\n\n## Jetpac: A Simple Way to Manage Data & Connectivity Costs\n\nTraditional roaming plans can become expensive quickly, especially during long-term stays. That’s why many travelers and new residents prefer using flexible digital solutions that keep their data costs predictable.\n\nJetpac offers an easy way to manage connectivity without hunting for local SIM cards or switching numbers, giving you a smooth online experience whether you are commuting, road tripping, or working remotely. Here’s why a Jetpac [New Zealand eSIM](https://www.jetpacglobal.com/product-details/new-zealand-esim) is the top choice:  \n  \nJetpac offers a simple way to manage connectivity costs by letting you set up one eSIM that works across more than 200 destinations, so you never have to juggle multiple SIM cards.\n\nIts 5G multi-network system keeps your phone connected even during long drives or remote area travel, and the prepaid pricing structure is designed to prevent bill shock by staying roughly 70 percent cheaper than traditional roaming.\n\nYou can share your data freely through unlimited hotspot access, and essential apps like WhatsApp, Uber, and Google Maps continue working even when your main data balance runs low.\n\nIf you ever need help, Jetpac’s support team is available around the clock via WhatsApp or email.\n\nIt even offers affordable international calling in more than 50 countries, starting at $1.99 for five minutes. Altogether, Jetpac keeps your mobile experience smooth, predictable, and stress-free wherever your trip takes you.\n\n## Save up to **70%** on **roaming data**\n\nNo hidden fees. No bill shock.\n\n![Jetpac roaming eSIM](https://content.jetpacglobal.com/web-images/destination-snippet-background.png)\n\n## FAQs\n\n### Are salaries in New Zealand enough to match the cost of living?\n\nSalaries vary widely depending on your industry, skill level, and the city you choose. In most regions, wages comfortably cover basic expenses, but places like Auckland and Wellington require mindful budgeting due to higher rent. Many newcomers adjust well once they understand which costs take up the largest portion of their income.\n\n### Is it cheaper to live in the North Island or the South Island?\n\nGenerally, the South Island is more affordable, with cities like Dunedin and Invercargill offering lower rent and slower-paced living. The North Island has more job opportunities, especially in Auckland and Wellington, but costs rise accordingly. Choosing between them often comes down to career options and lifestyle preferences, not just price.\n\n### What cost do newcomers almost always underestimate?\n\nGrocery and transport expenses tend to surprise most people. Supermarket prices, fuel, and dining out can significantly raise the average cost of living in New Zealand if not planned for. Many new residents learn to shop strategically and reduce trips to high-cost grocery chains.\n\n### How much should I save before relocating to New Zealand?\n\nA practical buffer is at least three months of living expenses. Individuals generally need between $4000 and $7000 USD, depending on housing choices and early setup costs. This safety net helps cover temporary accommodation, bond payments, transport, and essential household purchases.\n\n### Do rental homes usually come furnished?\n\nMost long-term rentals in New Zealand are unfurnished, especially in major cities. Tenants often need to buy basic furniture and kitchenware, which adds to initial expenses. Many residents cut these costs by using second-hand marketplaces, recycling shops, or local community groups.\n\n### Is it possible to live in New Zealand without a car?\n\nYes, but mainly in larger cities with reliable public transport, such as Wellington or Auckland. In smaller towns and rural areas, owning a car becomes almost essential for work, errands, and daily commuting. Transport choice affects the overall cost of living in New Zealand, so consider this when choosing where to live.\n\n### What’s the easiest way to stay connected after moving to New Zealand?\n\nMany newcomers start with flexible options like an [eSIM](https://www.jetpacglobal.com/product-details/new-zealand-esim/) while they settle in, especially if they are still choosing between mobile providers. Brands like [Jetpac](https://www.jetpacglobal.com/) help you stay online from day one, offering quick setup and coverage that works across New Zealand and other countries if you plan to travel during your move. It’s a convenient way to manage connectivity before committing to a long-term mobile plan.\n\n### Disclaimer:\n\n*All information in this blog reflects details available at the time of writing. Actual prices may change due to inflation, market shifts, or regional differences. Jetpac is not responsible for updates or variations that occur after publication.*\n\nGet connected as soon as you land with a Jetpac travel eSIM.\n\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w600/2026/10/Top-10-Cities-Near-Toronto-to-Visit.webp)\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w600/2026/10/Italy---s-Most-Livable-Cities-for-Nomads.webp)\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w600/2026/10/France-Transportation-Travel-Guide.webp)\n\nFind an eSIM plan for your next destination.\n\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w100/2026/06/ChatGPT-Image-Jun-18--2026--08_19_52-PM.png)\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w100/2026/01/052d24cfc82851e94d9ee0426a895ce0.png)\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w100/2026/01/9f71283bb793e7ffc4a0c562edf99d3b.jpg)\n\nFind an eSIM plan for your next destination.\n\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w100/2026/06/ChatGPT-Image-Jun-18--2026--08_19_52-PM.png)\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w100/2026/01/052d24cfc82851e94d9ee0426a895ce0.png)\n![](https://storage.ghost.io/c/1d/53/1d53f4e8-83ba-42fc-b33d-7ff10eabfcbb/content/images/size/w100/2026/01/9f71283bb793e7ffc4a0c562edf99d3b.jpg)\n![Jetpac logo](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/footer/jetpac-logo.svg?v=9q-NdNLrVJw-VW6g)\n\nTop destinations\n\nProducts\n\nResources\n\nSupport\n\n![Download on the App Store](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/footer/app-store-badge.svg?v=tWDvIo5D0RoeVy_X)\n![Get it on Google Play](https://blog.jetpacglobal.com/assets/footer/google-play-badge.svg?v=pqSg220OT7oj9I1y)",
